morphisms: The functor
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}
sends a morphism
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
in
G\mathbf{BC}
to the functor

f_{*}\colon\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X)\to\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X^{\prime})
defined as follows:

(a)

objects:
f_{*}(C,\rho,\mu)\coloneqq(C,\rho,f_{*}\mu)
.

(b)

morphisms:
f_{*}(A)\coloneqq A
.

For the verification that
f_{*}
is well-defined
we again refer to
[
BEc
, Sec. 4]
. It is at this point where we need the assumption that
{\mathbf{C}}
is effectively additive.
Following
[
BEL
, Def. 1.8]
we let
\mathrm{KK}
denote the presentable stable
\infty
-category
representing the finitary extension of
Kasparov’s
K\!K
-theory from separable to all small
C^{*}
-algebras.
We furthermore consider the functor
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathrm{KK}
defined in
[
BEL
, Def. 1.29]
. We then define
the topological
K
-theory functor for
C^{*}
-categories by

K^{C^{*}\mathrm{Cat}}\coloneqq\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},-)\colon C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}\,,

(3.3)

where we use the abbreviation

\mathrm{KK}(-,-)\coloneqq{\mathtt{map}}_{\mathrm{KK}}(\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}(-),\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}(-))\colon(C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})^{\mathrm{op}}\times C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\to\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}\,.

The functor (
3.3
) is equivalent to the functors considered in
[
Joa03
]
,
[
BE20b
, Sec. 8.5]
,
[
BEa
, Sec. 14]
.
Note that here we consider
C^{*}
-algebras like
{\mathbb{C}}
as
C^{*}
-categories with a single object.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
be effectively additive.

Definition 3.4
.

We define the functor
K{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
as the composition

K{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}\colon G\mathbf{BC}\xrightarrow{\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}}C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\xrightarrow{K^{C^{*}\mathrm{Cat}}}\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}\,.

For the definition of the notion of
an equivariant coarse homology theory and its additional properties we refer to
[
BEKW20a
]
. The following theorem is shown in
[
BEc
, Sec. 6]
(and
[
BEc
, Sec. 11]
for strong additivity).

Theorem 3.5
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
is effectively additive and admits countable AV-sums, then

K{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
is an equivariant coarse homology theory which is in addition strong and continuous. If
{\mathbf{C}}
admits all very small AV-sums, then
K{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
is strongly additive.

By construction the functors
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}
and
K{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
depend functorially on the coefficient category
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{ndeg},\mathrm{eadd},\omega\mathrm{add}})
.

4
\bm{G}
-uniform bornological coarse spaces, cones and
\bm{K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}}

A
G
-uniform structure on
X
is a
G
-invariant subset
{\mathcal{U}}
of
\mathcal{P}_{X\times X}
consisting of entourages containing the diagonal, which
is closed under taking supersets, finite intersections and the involution, and which has the property that every
U
in
{\mathcal{U}}
contains a
G
-invariant element of
{\mathcal{U}}
and admits
V
in
{\mathcal{U}}
with
V\circ V\subseteq U
. A
G
-uniform space is a pair
(X,{\mathcal{U}})
of a
G
-set and a
G
-uniform structure.
Let
(X,{\mathcal{U}})
and
(X^{\prime},{\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})
be
G
-uniform spaces and
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
be a
G
-invariant map of the underlying sets. Then
f
is uniform if
(f\times f)^{-1}({\mathcal{U}}^{\prime})\subseteq{\mathcal{U}}
.
A uniform structure
{\mathcal{U}}
is compatible with a coarse structure if
{\mathcal{U}}\cap{\mathcal{C}}\not=\emptyset
.

Let
G\mathbf{UBC}
denote the category of
G
-uniform bornological coarse spaces introduced in
[
BEKW20a
]
. Objects are tuples
(X,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{B}},{\mathcal{U}})
such that
(X,{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{B}})
is a
G
-bornological coarse space and
{\mathcal{U}}
is a
G
-uniform structure compatible with
{\mathcal{C}}
. Morphisms are morphisms of
G
-bornological coarse spaces which are in addition uniform. We will usually use the shorter notation
X
for
G
-uniform bornological coarse spaces.
We have canonical forgetful functors

G\mathbf{UBC}\to G\mathbf{BC}\ ,\quad G\mathbf{UBC}\to G{\mathbf{Top}}

(4.1)

which forget the uniform structure or take the underlying
G
-topological space, respectively.

If not said differently we will consider all subsets of
{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
as objects of
G\mathbf{UBC}
with the
trivial
G
-action and the structures induced by the standard metric.

The categories
G\mathbf{BC}
and
G\mathbf{UBC}
have monoidal structures
\otimes
which are the cartesian structure on the underlying
G
-uniform and
G
-coarse spaces (see
[
BEKW20a
, Ex. 2.17]
for the case of
G\mathbf{BC}
) such that the forgetful functor

G\mathbf{UBC}\to G\mathbf{BC}
is symmetric monoidal in the canonical way.
The bornology on
X\otimes X^{\prime}
is generated by the subsets
B\times B^{\prime}
for all bounded subsets
B
of
X
and
B^{\prime}
of
X^{\prime}
, respectively.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{UBC}
.

Definition 4.1
.

X
is flasque if it is a retract of
[0,\infty)\otimes X
.

Note that this definition is a little more restrictive than the definition given in
[
BE20a
, Text before Def. 3.10]
.
The same argument as for
[
BE20b
, Lem. 3.28]
in the non-equivariant case shows that
the underlying
G
-bornological coarse space of
X
is flasque in the generalized sense.

The notion of homotopy in the category
G\mathbf{UBC}
is defined in the usual manner using the interval
functor
X\mapsto[0,1]\otimes X
.

Recall the definitions of uniformly or coarsely excisive pairs from
[
BE20a
, Def. 3.3]
and
[
BE20a
, Def. 3.5]
.

Let
E\colon G\mathbf{UBC}\to\mathbf{M}
be a functor whose target is a stable
\infty
-category.

Definition 4.2
.

1.

E
is homotopy invariant if it sends the projection
[0,1]\otimes X\to X
to an equivalence for every
X
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
.

2.

E
satisfies closed excision if
E(\emptyset)\simeq 0
and for every uniformly and coarsely excisive pair
(Y,Z)
of invariant closed subsets of some
X
in
G\mathbf{UBC}

such that
X=Y\cup Z
the square

\textstyle{E(Y\cap Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(Y)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(Z)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{E(X)}

is a push-out square.

3.

E
vanishes on flasques if
E(X)\simeq 0
for any flasque
X
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
.

4.

E
is
u
-continuous if for every
X
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
we have
\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{V}E(X_{V})\simeq E(X)
, where
V
runs over
{\mathcal{C}}^{G}\cap{\mathcal{U}}
,
and
X_{V}
is obtained from
X
by replacing its coarse structure
{\mathcal{C}}
on
X
by the coarse structure generated by
V
.

In the following we describe two variants of the cone functor from
G\mathbf{UBC}
to
G\mathbf{BC}
.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{UBC}
with uniform structure
{\mathcal{U}}
.
Note that
{\mathcal{U}}
and
{\mathcal{P}_{X\times X}}
are posets with respect to the inclusion relation.

Definition 4.3
.

A scale for
X
is a pair
\tau\coloneqq(\phi,\psi)
, where:

1.

\phi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to(0,\infty)
is a bounded, non-increasing function such that
\phi_{|(-\infty,0]}\equiv 1
.

2.

\psi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to\mathcal{P}(X\times X)^{G}
is a non-increasing function with the following properties:

(a)

If
t
is in
(-\infty,0]
, then
\psi(t)=X\times X
.

(b)

For every
V
in
{\mathcal{U}}
there exists
t_{0}
in
{\mathbb{R}}
such that
\psi(t)\subseteq V
for all
t
in
[t_{0},\infty)
.

Definition 4.4
.

We define the geometric cone-at-
\infty
of
X
to be the object
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
in
G\mathbf{BC}
given as follows:

1.

The underlying
G
-set of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
is
{\mathbb{R}}\times X
.

2.

The bornology of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
is generated by the subsets
[-r,r]\times B
for all
r
in
(0,\infty)
and bounded subsets
B
of
X
.

3.

The coarse structure is generated by the entourages
U\cap U_{\tau}
for all scales
\tau\coloneqq(\phi,\psi)
with
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{t\to\infty}\phi(t)=0
, where
U
is a coarse entourage of
{\mathbb{R}}\otimes X
and

\displaystyle\mathclap{U_{\tau}\coloneqq\{((s,x),(t,y))\in({\mathbb{R}}\times X)\times({\mathbb{R}}\times X)\>|\>|s-t|\leq\phi(\max\{s,t\}),(x,y)\in\psi(\max\{s,t\})\}\,.}

We furthermore define the cone
{\mathcal{O}}(X)
of
X
to be the subset
[0,\infty)\times X
of
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
with the induced structures.

In particular, in the case of
X=*
the choice of
\psi
is irrelevant and we consider

U_{\phi}\coloneqq\{(s,t)\in{\mathbb{R}}\>|\>|s-t|\leq\phi(\max\{s,t\})\}\,.

(4.3)

Definition 4.5
.

We define functors

{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty},{\mathcal{O}}\colon G\mathbf{UBC}\to G\mathbf{BC}

as follows:

1.

objects: The functors send
X
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
to
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
or
{\mathcal{O}}(X)
, respectively.

2.

morphisms: The functors send a morphism
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
to the morphism

{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X^{\prime})
or
{\mathcal{O}}(X)\to{\mathcal{O}}(X^{\prime})
given by

{\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathbb{R}}}\times f
or
{\mathtt{id}}_{[0,\infty)}\times f
, respectively.

The definition of the functors for morphisms in Point
2
needs a justification which is given e.g. by a specialization of the argument for
[
BE20b
, Lem. 5.15]
.

For
X
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
we have a natural sequence of maps in
G\mathbf{UBC}

X\to{\mathcal{O}}(X)\to{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\to{\mathbb{R}}\otimes X

(4.4)

called the cone sequence. Here the first map is given by
x\mapsto(0,x)
, the second map is the inclusion, and the third map is the identity on the underlying sets.

Let
E\colon G\mathbf{BC}\to\mathbf{M}
be a functor with target a stable
\infty
-category.
Then we consider the functors

\displaystyle E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}

\displaystyle\coloneqq E\circ{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}\colon G\mathbf{UBC}\to\mathbf{M}

(4.5)

\displaystyle E{\mathcal{O}}

\displaystyle\coloneqq E\circ{\mathcal{O}}\colon G\mathbf{UBC}\to\mathbf{M}\,.

Proposition 4.6
.

We assume that
E
is a coarse homology theory which is in addition strong.
Then the functors
E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}
and
E{\mathcal{O}}
have the following properties:

1.

homotopy invariance,

2.

closed excision,

3.

vanishing on flasques,

4.

u
-continuous.

Moreover, the cone sequence (
4.4
) induces a fibre sequence of functors

E\to E{\mathcal{O}}\to E{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}\xrightarrow{\partial^{{\mathtt{Cone}}}}\Sigma E\,.

(4.6)

This proposition follows from the results stated in
[
BE20a
, Sec. 9]
(which are stated there in the non-equivariant case, but the same proof applies here). In particular, the list of properties of the functors is given by
[
BE20a
, Lem. 9.6]
and
the cone sequence follows from
[
BE20a
, (9.1)]
.
Note that we consider
E
in (
4.6
) as a functor on
G\mathbf{UBC}
by using the first forgetful functor in (
4.1
).

Let
Y
be in
G\mathbf{BC}
and
E\colon G\mathbf{BC}\to\mathbf{M}
be some functor.

Definition 4.7

(
[
BEKW20a
, (10.17)]
)
.

We define the twist
E_{Y}
of
E
by
Y
as the functor

E_{Y}\colon G\mathbf{BC}\to\mathbf{M}\,,\quad E_{Y}(X)\coloneqq E(X\otimes Y)\,.

The following has been shown in
[
BEKW20a
, Lem. 4.17 & 11.25]
:

Lemma 4.8
.

If
E
is a coarse homology theory, then so is its twist
E_{Y}
.
If
E
is strong, then so is
E_{Y}
.

We apply this construction to the equivariant coarse homology theory
K{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
from Definition
3.4
.
The group
G
gives rise to the
G
-bornological coarse spaces

G_{can,min}
and
G_{can,max}

[
BEKW20a
, Ex. 2.4]
.
Here
min
and
max
refer to the minimal (finite subsets) and maximal (all subsets) bornologies, and the canonical coarse structure
can

is the minimal
G
-coarse structure such that
G_{can}
is a connected
G
-coarse space. It is generated by the entourages

\{(g,h)\}
for all
(g,h)
in
G\times G
.
Later we will in particular consider the coarse homology theories

K{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c,G_{can,max}}
and
K{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c,G_{can,min}}
obtained from

K{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c}
by twisting with
G_{can,max}
and
G_{can,min}
, respectively.

Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
be effectively additive.

Definition 4.9
.

We define the functor

K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}\colon G\mathbf{UBC}\to\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}

as the composition

K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}\colon G\mathbf{UBC}\xrightarrow{{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}}G\mathbf{BC}\xrightarrow{K{\mathbf{C}}{\mathcal{X}}^{G}_{c,G_{can,max}}}\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}\,.

The following proposition lists the properties of the functor
K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}
.
It is a consequence of Theorem
3.5
and Proposition
4.6
. It should be compared with
the properties of the functor
K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}
from (
1.2
) which are listed in
[
BEL
, Prop. 1.15]
.

Proposition 4.10
.

If
{\mathbf{C}}
is effectively additive and admits all countable AV-sums, then
the functor
K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}
has the following properties:

1.

closed excision,

2.

homotopy invariant,

3.

u
-continuous,

4.

vanishing on flasques.

The functor
K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}
depends functorially on coefficient category
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{ndeg},\mathrm{eadd},\omega\mathrm{add}})
.

5
Preparation of the statement of Theorem
1.4

In this section we will introduce the constructions going into the statement of our main Theorem
1.4

about Paschke duality relating the functors

K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}
and
K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}
with each other. Note that these functors are defined on different domains, namely
G\mathbf{LCH}^{\mathrm{prop}}_{+}
(recall that this is the category of all locally compact
G
-spaces and partially defined proper maps) and
G\mathbf{UBC}
. Hence before we can construct a natural transformation between these functors we must restrict both functors to a common domain.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{UBC}
.

Definition 5.1
.

We define
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
and
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(X)
in
G\mathbf{BC}
as in Definition
4.4
but dropping the condition that
\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{t\to\infty}\phi(t)=0
in Definition
4.4
.
3
.

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{UBC}
and let
\tau=(\phi,\psi)
be a scale for
X
as (Definition
4.3
).

Definition 5.2
.

We say that
\tau
is uniform if
\psi
takes values in the uniform structure of
X
.

Note that if the uniform structure of
X
does not admit a countable basis, then
X
does not admit any uniform scale.

Let
\tau
be a uniform scale on
X
and recall the definition (
) of the entourage
U_{\tau}
on
{\mathbb{R}}\times X
.

Definition 5.3
.

1.

We let
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{\tau}(X)
be the
G
-bornological coarse space
obtained from
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)
by replacing its coarse structure by the coarse structure generated by all entourages of the form
U\cap U_{\tau}
for all coarse entourages
U
of
{\mathbb{R}}\otimes X
.

2.

We let
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)
be the subset
[0,\infty)\times X
with the structures induced from
{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{\tau}(X)
.

Let
\tau,\tau^{\prime}
be uniform scales for
X
and assume that
\tau\geq\tau^{\prime}
, i.e.,
\phi\geq\phi^{\prime}
and
\psi\supseteq\psi^{\prime}
. Then
the identity of the underlying sets are morphisms

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}_{\tau^{\prime}}(X)\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}^{\infty}(X)\to\tilde{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)\leftarrow{\mathcal{O}}^{\infty}(X)

(5.1)

\displaystyle{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau^{\prime}}(X)\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\to\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(X)\leftarrow{\mathcal{O}}(X)

in
G\mathbf{BC}
.

Proposition 5.4
.

The morphisms in (
5.1
) are sent to equivalences by every coarse homology theory.

Proof.

The proofs of the results in
[
BE20a
, Sec. 8]
apply also to the equivariant case without change.
∎

Let
X
be in
G\mathbf{UBC}
and

\tau
be a uniform scale for
X
.
Then we can form
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
in
G\mathbf{BC}
.
Let
L
be a subset of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
.

Definition 5.5
.

L
is locally uniformly finite if for every
bounded subset
B
of
X
and coarse entourage
U
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
we have

\sup_{y\in[0,\infty)\times B\times G}|L\cap U[\{y\}]|<\infty\,.

Definition 5.6
.

We define the category
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
as follows:

1.

objects: The objects of
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}

are pairs
(X,\tau)
of
X
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
and a uniform scale
\tau
such that:

(a)

The underlying topological space of
X

is locally compact, Hausdorff, and second countable.

(b)

The bornology of
X
consists of the relatively compact subsets.

(c)

{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
admits an invariant locally uniformly finite subset
L
such that
L\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
is a coarse equivalence.

2.

A morphism
f\colon(X,\tau)\to(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
is a morphism
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
such that there exists an integer
m
such that
(f\times f)\circ\psi\subseteq(\psi^{\prime})^{m}
and
U_{\phi}\subseteq U_{\phi^{\prime}}^{m}
(see (
4.3
) for notation), where
\tau=(\phi,\psi)
and
\tau^{\prime}=(\phi^{\prime},\psi^{\prime})
.

Here for an entourage
U
we let
\smash{U^{m}\coloneqq\underbrace{U\circ\dots\circ U}_{m\text{-times}}}
denote the
m
-fold composition of
U
with itself.

Remark 5.7
.

The reason for working with fixed scales and not with
\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(X)
or
{\mathcal{O}}(X)
is that we do not expect that

\tilde{\mathcal{O}}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
or
{\mathcal{O}}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
satisfy the analogue of Condition
5.6
.
1c
.
But this condition is crucial for the construction of the Paschke morphism. It goes into the proof of Lemma
6.2
below.

∎

In the following discussion we provide some examples for objects in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
.
The existence of such objects is not completely obvious because of Condition
5.6
.
1c
.

Let
G\mathbf{Simpl}
be the category of
G
-simplicial complexes and equivariant simplicial maps.
We will consider
G
-simplicial complexes as metric spaces with the spherical path quasi-metric. The latter is uniquely characterized by the property that it induces the spherical metric on simplices, i.e., the metric induced from the Riemannian metric on the standard spheres
S^{n}

by identifying the
n
-simplices with the intersection
S^{n}\cap[0,\infty)^{n+1}
.

Definition 5.8
.

A
G
-simplicial complex has bounded geometry if there is a uniform bound on the number of simplices in the stars of the vertices.

Note that if a
G
-simplicial complex
K
has bounded geometry, then
K
is locally finite and finite-dimensional.

We let
G\mathbf{Simpl}_{{\mathrm{bg}}}^{\mathrm{prop}}
be the subcategory of
G\mathbf{Simpl}
of
G
-simplicial complexes of bounded geometry and proper maps.

Let
X
be a metric space with an isometric
G
-action. For
r
in
[0,\infty)
and
x
in
X
we let
B_{r}(x)
denote the closed metric ball of radius
r
with center
x
.

Definition 5.9
.

We say
X
has locally uniformly bounded geometry if it admits a family of invariant subsets
(Y_{s})_{s\in(0,\infty)}
such that:

1.

Y_{s}
is
s
-dense in
X
, i.e.,
\bigcup_{y\in Y}B_{s}(y)=X
.

2.

For every
n
in
{\rm Nature}
and compact subset
B
of
X
we have

\sup_{s\in(0,\infty)}\sup_{x\in X}|Y_{s}\cap B\cap B_{ns}(x)|<\infty\,.

(5.2)

This definition is weaker than

[
EWZ
, Def. 4.1]
since we
require the uniformity conditions (
5.2
) only on compact subsets of
X
.

Example 5.10
.

A complete Riemannian manifold with isometric
G
-action
has locally uniformly bounded geometry. The argument of
[
Wul19
, Lem. 5.2]

applies without the bounded geometry assumption on the manifold since we restrict to compact subsets.

The objects of
G\mathbf{Simpl}^{\mathrm{prop}}_{{\mathrm{bg}}}
have locally uniformly bounded geometry.
Given
s
in
(0,\infty)
, the subset
Y_{s}
can be chosen as
zero skeleton of a sufficiently iterated barycentric subdivision such that Condition
5.9
.
1
is satisfied. Condition
5.9
.
2
is then automatic by the bounded geometry assumption.

∎

If
(X,d)
is a quasi-metric space, then we consider the function

\psi_{d}\colon[0,\infty)\to\mathcal{P}(X\times X)

given by

\psi_{d}(t)\coloneqq\{(x,x^{\prime})\in X\times X\>|\>d(x,x^{\prime})\leq t^{-1}\}\,.

Then
\tau_{d}\coloneqq({\mathtt{const}}_{1},\psi_{d})
is a uniform scale.
We will equip metric spaces with the uniform bornological coarse structure induced by the metric.

Proposition 5.11
.

1.

If

(X,d)
is a proper metric space with an isometric
G
-action and locally uniformly
bounded geometry,
then
(X,\tau_{d})
belongs to
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
.

2.

There is a functor

G\mathbf{Simpl}^{\mathrm{prop}}_{{\mathrm{bg}}}\to G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
which sends

X
in
G\mathbf{Simpl}^{\mathrm{prop}}_{{\mathrm{bg}}}
to
(X,\tau_{d})
.

Proof.

In order to show Assertion
1

the only non-obvious condition to check is that

(X,\tau_{d})
satisfies
5.6
.
1c
.
We define the
G
-invariant subset
L
of
[0,\infty)\times X\times G
as follows. Let
(Y_{s})_{s\in(0,\infty)}
be a family of subsets of
X
as in Definition
5.9
.
For
n
in
{\rm Nature}\setminus\{0\}
we choose the
n^{-1}
-dense subset
L_{n,0}\coloneqq Y_{n^{-1}}
of
X
.
Then we define the
G
-invariant subset

L_{n}\coloneqq\{(g\ell,gh)\>|\>g,h\in G\text{ and }\ell\in L_{n,0}\}

of
X\times G
. Finally we define the
G
-invariant subset

L\coloneqq\bigcup_{n\in{\rm Nature}\setminus\{0\}}\{n\}\times L_{n}

of
[0,\infty)\times X\times G
.
We consider the entourage

\displaystyle V_{m}\coloneqq\{((s,x),(s^{\prime},x^{\prime}))\in{([0,\infty)\times X)^{2}}\>|\>|s-s^{\prime}|\leq m,d(x,x^{\prime})\leq m\min\{1,s^{-1},s^{\prime,-1}\}\}

of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)
, and for every finite subset
F
of
G
we define the entourage
W_{F}\coloneqq G(F\times F)
of
G_{can,max}
.

By an inspection we see that

L
is
V_{1}\times W_{\{e\}}
-dense.
Since
G
acts freely on
L
and on
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
it follows that
the inclusion
L\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
is a coarse equivalence.

It suffices to verify the local uniform finiteness condition in Definition
5.5
for
L
only for the cofinal set of entourages
V_{m}\times W_{F}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
, where
F
runs
over all finite subsets
F
of
G
and
m
is in
{\rm Nature}
.

Let
B
be a compact subset of
X
.
Let
(s,x,g)
be in
[0,\infty)\times X\times G
.

Assume that
n
is the largest integer smaller than
s-m
.
If
(s^{\prime},x^{\prime},g^{\prime})
is in the subset
(V_{m}\times W_{F})[\{(s,x,g)\}]
, then

|s^{\prime}-s|\leq m
,
d(x^{\prime},x)\leq m\min\{1,n^{-1}\}
and
g^{-1}g^{\prime}\in F^{-1}F
.
We conclude that

|(V_{m}\times W_{F})[\{(s,x,g)\}]\cap L\cap([0,\infty)\times B\times G)|

is bounded by

|F^{-1}F|\cdot\sum_{k=n}^{n+2m}|Y_{k^{-1}}\cap B\cap B_{m\min\{1,n^{-1}\}}(x^{\prime})|
.
Using Definition
5.9
.
2
we see that this number is bounded independently of
(s,x,g)
.

We now show the Assertion
2
.
If
X
is in
G\mathbf{Simpl}_{{\mathrm{bg}}}^{\mathrm{prop}}
, then
X
is a metric space with an isometric
G
-action and locally uniformly bounded geometry. Therefore

(X,\tau_{d})
is in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
.
If
f\colon X\to X^{\prime}
is a morphism in
G\mathbf{Simpl}_{{\mathrm{bg}}}^{\mathrm{prop}}
, then

f
is a contraction and proper. Consequently it is a morphism in
G\mathbf{UBC}
. Furthermore,
the conditions in Definition
5.6
.
2
are satisfied with
m=1
.
∎

The following lemma is again not completely obvious because of Condition
5.6
.
1c
.

Lemma 5.12
.

The category
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
is closed under taking subspaces with the induced structures and scales.

Proof.

Let
(X,\tau)
be in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
and let
Y
be an invariant subset of
X
.
We consider
Y
as an object of
G\mathbf{UBC}
by equipping it with the induced structures.
Further, we can restrict the uniform scale
\tau
to a uniform scale
\tau_{Y}
on
Y
: if
\tau=(\phi,\psi)
, then
\tau_{Y}=(\phi,\psi\cap(Y\times Y))
.
We must show that
(Y,\tau)
is in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
.

The only non-trivial part is to check the condition of Definition
5.6
.
1c
, i.e. the existence of a locally uniformly finite subset
L_{Y}
in

{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{Y}}(Y)\otimes G_{can,max}
such that
L_{Y}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{Y}}(Y)\otimes G_{can,max}
is a coarse equivalence.

By assumption there exists such a subset
L
in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
.
Let
U
be an invariant entourage of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
such that
L
is
U
-dense.
For every
G
-orbit
G\ell
in
L/G
represented by
\ell
in
L

such that
U[\ell]\cap({\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{Y}}(Y)\otimes G_{can,max})\not=\emptyset
we can choose an
\ell^{\prime}
in
U[\ell]\cap({\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{Y}}(Y)\otimes G_{can,max})
. We then let
L_{Y}
be the union of these
G
-orbits
G\ell^{\prime}
.

Then
L_{Y}
is
U^{2}
-dense in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{Y}}(Y)\otimes G_{can,max}
and hence

L_{Y}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{Y}}(Y)\otimes G_{can,max}
is a coarse equivalence.

For every bounded subset
B
of
Y
and
y
in
Y
we have

|L_{Y}\cap B\cap V[\{y\}]|\leq|L\cap U[B]\cap UV[\{y\}]|\,,

and this number is uniformly bounded with respect to
y
in
Y
.
∎

We have canonical forgetful functors

\iota^{\mathrm{top}}\colon G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}\to G\mathbf{LCH}^{\mathrm{prop}}_{+}\quad\text{ and }\quad\iota^{\mathrm{scl}}\colon G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}\to G\mathbf{UBC}

(5.3)

which send
(X,\tau)
to its underlying
G
-topological space or forget the scale, respectively.

Let
(X,\tau),(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
be in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
and
f_{0},f_{1}\colon(X,\tau)\to(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
be two morphisms. The following notion of homotopy will be used in Proposition
9.3
further below.

Definition 5.13
.

f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are homotopic if
\iota^{\mathrm{scl}}(f_{0})
and
\iota^{\mathrm{scl}}(f_{1})
are homotopic.

6
Locality and pseudolocality

We have a functor

G\mathbf{LCH}^{\mathrm{prop}}\to G\mathbf{Born}

sending a locally compact Hausdorff

G
-topological space to the underlying
G
-bornological space with the bornology of the relatively compact subsets.
This functor will usually be dropped from the notation.
Note that this functor does not extend to the larger category
G\mathbf{LCH}^{\mathrm{prop}}_{+}
containing maps which are only partially defined.

For
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
and

X
in
G\mathbf{LCH}^{\mathrm{prop}}
we form the
G
-
C^{*}
-category

{\mathbf{C}}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{lf}}([0,\infty)\otimes X\otimes G_{max})
introduced in Definition
2.14
which only depends on the underlying
G
-bornological space of
X
.
We consider an object
(C,\rho,\mu)
in

{\mathbf{C}}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{lf}}([0,\infty)\otimes X\otimes G_{max})
.
We then extend the projection-valued measure
\mu
to a homomorphism of
C^{*}
-algebras
\phi\colon C_{0}(X)\to{\mathtt{End}}_{{\mathbf{M}}{\mathbf{C}}}(C)

which sends
f
in
C_{0}(X)
to

\phi(f)\coloneqq\int_{[0,\infty)\times X\times G}\pi^{*}(f)d\mu\,,

(6.1)

where

\pi\colon[0,\infty)\times X\times G\to X

(6.2)

is the projection.

Remark 6.1
.

This integral can be interpreted as follows.
For every
y
in
[0,\infty)\times X\times G
we can choose a representative

u_{y}\colon C_{y}\to C
of the image in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
of the projection
\mu(\{y\})
on
C
. By Definition
2.12
.
2c

(C,(u_{y})_{y\in[0,\infty)\times X\times G})

(6.3)

represents the orthogonal sum of the family
(C_{y})_{y\in[0,\infty)\times X\times G}
.
Using that
f
is bounded and that the family
(u_{y})_{y\in[0,\infty)\times X\times G}
is mutually orthogonal we conclude using
[
BEa
, Lem. 7.8]
that the sum

\phi(f):=\sum_{y\in[0,\infty)\times X\times G}u_{y}f(\pi(y))u_{y}^{*}

strictly converges in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
.

∎

One checks that
\phi
is a homomorphism of
C^{*}
-algebras. Furthermore, using the equivariance (
2.3
) of
\mu
, one checks that
\phi
is equivariant in the sense that

g^{-1}\cdot\phi(f)=\phi(g^{*}f)

(6.4)

for all
g
in
G
, see (
2.2
) for notation.

For the rest of this subsection, let
(X,\tau)
be an object of the category
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
described in Definition
5.6
. Recall that
the underlying
G
-topological space of
X
is Hausdorff, locally compact and second countable.
We then have the cone
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)
in
G\mathbf{BC}
introduced in Definition
5.3
. Let
{\mathbf{C}}
be in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
and recall
the Definition
3.2
of the
C^{*}
-category

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max})
. Any object
(C,\rho,\mu)
of this category is also an object of

{\mathbf{C}}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{lf}}([0,\infty)\otimes X\otimes G_{max})

so that for
f
in
C_{0}(X)
the endomorphism
\phi(f)
in
{\mathtt{End}}_{\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}}(C)
is defined by (
6.1
).

Let
A\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be a morphism in
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max})
.
Recall that
A
is in particular a multiplier morphism from
C
to
C^{\prime}
.
Our next result states that
A
is pseudolocal (in the sense of
[
HR00
, Def. 12.3.1]
if one replaces the ideal of compact operators in all bounded operators by the ideal
{\mathbf{C}}
in the multiplier category
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
) when we consider the objects of
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max})
as
X
-controlled via (
6.1
).
Let
f
be in
C_{0}(X)
and
\phi^{\prime}
be defined as in (
6.1
), but for the object
(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
.

Lemma 6.2
.

The difference
A\phi(f)-\phi^{\prime}(f)A
belongs to
{\mathbf{C}}
.

Proof.

Assume that
A
is controlled by a symmetric entourage
U
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes{G_{can,max}}
, and that

f
is in
C_{c}(X)
. We fix
\epsilon
in
(0,\infty)
.

By Assumption
5.6
.
1c
on
X
there exists a locally uniformly finite subset
L
(Definition
5.5
) such that the inclusion

i\colon L\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
is a coarse equivalence. Therefore we can choose an inverse morphism

j\colon{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}\to L
in
G\mathbf{BC}
such that
i\circ j
is
U^{\prime}
-close to the identity
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}

for some coarse entourage
U^{\prime}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
.
This gives an equivariant partition
(Y_{\ell})_{\ell\in L}
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
, were we set
Y_{\ell}\coloneqq j^{-1}(\{\ell\})
.

In view of the construction of the coarse structure on
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)
in Definition
4.4
.
3
the projection
\pi
in (
6.2
) is controlled, and hence
(\pi\times\pi)(U^{\prime}UU^{\prime})
a coarse entourage of
X
.
Because
{\mathtt{supp}}(f)
is compact, it is a bounded subset of
X
. By the compatibility of the bornology and the coarse structure of
X
its thickening

B\coloneqq(\pi\times\pi)(U^{\prime}UU^{\prime})[{\mathtt{supp}}(f)]

(6.5)

is a bounded subset of
X
, too.
We define the subset
F\coloneqq\pi^{-1}(B)
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
.

Since
L
is locally uniformly finite
we can define the (finite) integer

R\coloneqq\sup_{y\in F}|L\cap U^{\prime}U[\{y\}]|\,.

(6.6)

Since
{\mathtt{supp}}(f)
is compact and
f
is continuous, it is uniformly continuous. Hence there exists a uniform entourage

T
on
X
such that

|f(x)-f(x^{\prime})|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{3(1+\|A\|R)}

for all
(x,x^{\prime})
in
T
.

In view of Condition
4.3
.
2b
and Definition
4.4
, the fact that
U^{\prime,2}UU^{\prime,2}
belongs to the coarse structure of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
implies that there exists

r
in
(0,\infty)
sufficiently large such that

(\pi\times\pi)(U^{\prime,2}UU^{\prime,2}\cap(([0,\infty)\times X\times G)\times([r,\infty)\times F\times G))\subseteq T\,.

(6.7)

Let
t\colon[0,\infty)\times X\times G\to[0,\infty)
be the projection to the first coordinate.
Assume that
\ell,\ell^{\prime}
are in
L
such that
(\ell^{\prime},\ell)\in U^{\prime}UU^{\prime}
.
If
y^{\prime}
is in
Y_{\ell^{\prime}}
and
y
is in
Y_{\ell}
, then
(y^{\prime},y)\in U^{\prime,2}UU^{\prime,2}
.
If in addition

{y\in F}
and
t(y)\geq r
,
then
(\pi(y^{\prime}),\pi(y))\in T
by (
6.7
), and hence

|f(\pi(y))-f(\pi(y^{\prime}))|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{3(1+\|A\|R)}\,.

(6.8)

We consider the partition of
[0,\infty)\times X\times G
into the subsets

W\coloneqq\{{y\in[0,\infty)\times X\times G\>|\>r\leq t(y)}\}\text{ and }V\coloneqq\{{y\in[0,\infty)\times X\times G\>|\>0\leq t(y)<r}\}\,.

We have the following equality:

\displaystyle A\phi(f)-\phi^{\prime}(f)A

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!}}{{=}}

\displaystyle A\phi(f)\mu(F)-\phi^{\prime}(f)A\mu(F)

(6.9)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle!!}}{{=}}

\displaystyle A\phi(f)\mu(F\cap W)+A\phi(f)\mu(F\cap V)

\displaystyle-\phi^{\prime}(f)A\mu(F\cap W)-\phi^{\prime}(f)A\mu(F\cap V)

where for
!
we use that
\phi(f)\mu(F)=\phi(f)
since
{\mathtt{supp}}(f)\subseteq B
and therefore
{\mathtt{supp}}(\pi^{*}f)\subseteq F
, and that
A
is
U
-controlled and
X\setminus F
is
U
-separated from
{\mathtt{supp}}(\pi^{*}f)

by (
6.5
) and the definition of
F
. For
!!
we use the additivity of
\mu
.
Since
G_{can,max}
is bounded the subset
F\cap V
is bounded in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
.
Since
(C,\rho,\mu)
is locally finite (in particular by the two conditions
2.12
.
2b
and
2d
) the projection
\mu(F\cap V)
and hence the
morphisms

A\phi(f)\mu(F\cap V)
and
\phi^{\prime}(f)A\mu(F\cap V)
belong to
{{\mathbf{C}}}
.

We now show that the remaining part of (
6.9
) satisfies

\|A\phi(f)\mu(F\cap W)-\phi^{\prime}(f)A\mu(F\cap W)\|\leq\epsilon\,.

(6.10)

The morphism
A\phi(f)\mu(F\cap W)-\phi^{\prime}(f)A\mu(F\cap W)

determines and is determined by a matrix
(M_{\ell^{\prime}\ell})_{\ell^{\prime},\ell\in L}
of multiplier morphisms from
C
to
C^{\prime}

given by

M_{\ell^{\prime}\ell}:=\mu^{\prime}(Y_{\ell^{\prime}})[A\phi(f)\mu(F\cap W)-\phi^{\prime}(f)A\mu(F\cap W)]\mu(Y_{\ell})\ .

The sum

\sum_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}\in L}M_{\ell,\ell^{\prime}}
strictly converges to
A\phi(f)\mu(F\cap W)-\phi^{\prime}(f)A\mu(F\cap W)
.
For fixed
\ell
we estimate
the norm of the partial sum

\sum_{\ell^{\prime}\in L}M_{\ell^{\prime}\ell}\colon C\to C^{\prime}

(6.11)

by the sum of the norms of the summands.
If
\ell^{\prime}
contributes non-trivially to this sum, then
(\ell^{\prime},\ell)\in U^{\prime}UU^{\prime}
. The estimate (
6.8
) then implies that

\|M_{\ell^{\prime},\ell}\|\leq\frac{\epsilon\|A\|}{(1+\|A\|R)}\,.

The number of non-zero summands in (
6.11
) is bounded by
R
defined in (
6.6
).
2
2

2

The need to have such a bound on the number of summands is the reason for considering scales and locally uniformly finite subsets.

The whole sum in (
6.11
) is therefore bounded by
\epsilon
. Using the mutual orthogonality of the family of projections
{(\mu(Y_{\ell})})_{\ell\in L}
we conclude
the estimate (
6.10
).

Since we can choose
\epsilon
arbitrarily small and
{{\mathbf{C}}}
is closed in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}
we see that

A\phi(f)-\phi^{\prime}(f)A\in{{\mathbf{C}}}
.
Again since
{{\mathbf{C}}}
is closed in
\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}

we furthermore conclude that
A\phi(f)-\phi^{\prime}(f)A\in{{\mathbf{C}}}
also for all

f
in
C_{0}(X)
and
A
in

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max})}((C,\rho,\mu),(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}))
.
∎

If
Z
is an invariant subset of a
G
-bornological coarse space
Y
, then we define the wide subcategory

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(Z\subseteq Y)
of
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(Y)
(see
[
BEc
, Def. 5.5]
) such that
for objects
(C,\rho,\mu)
and
(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
in

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(Z\subseteq Y)

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(Z\subseteq Y)}((C,\rho,\mu),(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}))\coloneqq\mu^{\prime}(Z){\mathtt{Hom}}_{\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(Y)}((C,\rho,\mu),(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}))\mu(Z)\,.

Similarly, for an invariant big family
{\mathcal{Z}}=(Z_{i})_{I\in I}
on
Y
(see
[
BEKW20a
]
) we have the wide subcategory

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{Z}}\subseteq Y)\coloneqq\overline{\bigcup_{i\in I}\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(Z_{i}\subseteq Y)}

(6.12)

of
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(Y)
(the union and closure are both taken on the level of morphisms). By
[
BEc
, Lem. 5.9]
we know that
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{Z}}\subseteq Y)

is an ideal in
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(Y)
.

We apply this to the case
Y={\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}

and consider the big family

{\mathcal{Z}}\coloneqq(Z_{n})_{n\in{\rm Nature}}\,,\quad Z_{n}\coloneqq[0,n]\times X\times G\,.

(6.13)

Let
A\colon(C,\rho,\mu)\to(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})
be a morphism in
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{Z}}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max})
. Our next result shows that it locally belongs to
{{\mathbf{C}}}
.
Let
f
be in
C_{0}(X)
.

Lemma 6.3
.

We have
\phi^{\prime}(f)A\in{{\mathbf{C}}}
and
A\phi(f)\in{{\mathbf{C}}}
.

Proof.

It suffices to show that
\phi^{\prime}(f)A\in{{\mathbf{C}}}
.
In order to deduce
A\phi(f)\in{{\mathbf{C}}}
we then use the involution.

We first assume that there exists
n
in
{\rm Nature}
such that

\mu(Z_{n})A\mu(Z_{n})=A
.
If
f
is in
C_{c}(X)
, then
\mu(Z_{n}\cap\pi^{-1}({\mathtt{supp}}(f)))
belongs to
{{\mathbf{C}}}
since
(C,\rho,\mu)
is locally finite and
Z_{n}\cap\pi^{-1}({\mathtt{supp}}(f))
is bounded in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}
. Consequently, we conclude that

\phi^{\prime}(f)A=\phi^{\prime}(f)\mu^{\prime}(Z_{n}\cap\pi^{-1}({\mathtt{supp}}(f)))A

belongs to
{{\mathbf{C}}}
.
Since
{{\mathbf{C}}}
is closed in
{\mathbf{M}{\mathbf{C}}}
we see that
\phi^{\prime}(f)A\in{{\mathbf{C}}}
also for all

f
in
C_{0}(X)
and
A
in

{\mathtt{Hom}}_{\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{Z}}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max})}((C,\rho,\mu),(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}))\,.\qed

Remark 6.4
.

Note that the statement of Lemma
6.3
also holds more generally for any
X
in
G\mathbf{UBC}
and

{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}
replaced by
{\mathcal{O}}
.

7
Construction of the Paschke morphism

The main result of the present section is the description of the Paschke morphism for a given space
(X,\tau)
in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
.
The general idea for its construction via a multiplication map like
\mu_{(X,\tau)}
as below, but with completely different technical details otherwise, has been used at various places, see e.g.
[
WY20
, Sec. 6.5]
or
[
Wul
, Sec. 6.4]
.
In the Section
8
we will provide a refinement of this construction to a natural transformation of functors defined on
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
.

We start with a description of the following intermediate constructions which go into the construction of the Paschke morphism:

1.

The functor
(X,\tau)\mapsto\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)
from
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
to
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
,

2.

the tensor product
C_{0}(X)\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)
,

3.

the multiplication morphism
\mu_{(X,\tau)}\colon C_{0}(X)\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)\to{\mathbf{Q}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}}
,

4.

the diagonal morphism
\delta_{(X,\tau)}\colon\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X))\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(X)\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X))
.

We have a functor

G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}\to G\mathbf{BC}\,,\quad(X,\tau)\mapsto{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max}\,,

(7.1)

where
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)
is described in Definition
5.3
.
2
.
For an effectively additive
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
, composing (
7.1
) with
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}
from Definition
3.3
we get a functor

G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}\,,\quad(X,\tau)\mapsto{\mathbf{D}}_{\tau}(X)\coloneqq\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max})\,.

(7.2)

We furthermore have the subfunctor

G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\,,\quad(X,\tau)\mapsto{\mathbf{C}}_{\tau}(X)\coloneqq\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{Z}}\subseteq{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max})

(7.3)

(see (
6.12
) and (
6.13
) for notation)
such that
{\mathbf{C}}_{\tau}(X)
is a closed ideal in
{\mathbf{D}}_{\tau}(X)
.
Note that
{\mathbf{C}}_{\tau}(X)
is our replacement for

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,max})
which can be considered as a subcategory of

\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max})
of objects which are supported on
\{0\}\times X\times G
, but which is not an ideal (these two
C^{*}
-categories actually have the same
K
-theory as will be used and also explained further below in Diagram (
)).
Our choice of notation
{\mathbf{C}}_{\tau}(X)
and
{\mathbf{D}}_{\tau}(X)
should indicate that
these
C^{*}
-categories are our versions of the Roe algebra and the algebra of pseudolocal operators. We refer to Section
11
for more details.
By forming quotients of
C^{*}
-categories we finally define the functor

G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}\to C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}\,,\quad(X,\tau)\mapsto\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)\coloneqq\frac{{\mathbf{D}}_{\tau}(X)}{{\mathbf{C}}_{\tau}(X)}\,.

(7.4)

The functors
{\mathbf{C}}_{\tau}
,
{\mathbf{D}}_{\tau}
and
\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}
depend functorially on
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{ndeg},\mathrm{eadd},\omega\mathrm{add}})
since
\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}
has this property.

Recall the forgetful functor
\iota^{\mathrm{scl}}\colon G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}\to G\mathbf{UBC}
, the functor
K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}
from Definition
4.9
, and
the
K
-theory functor
K^{C^{*}\mathrm{Cat}}
for
C^{*}
-categories from (
3.3
).

We assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
is effectively additive and admits countable AV-sums.

Lemma 7.1
.

We have a canonical equivalence of functors

K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}\circ\iota^{\mathrm{scl}}\simeq K^{C^{*}\mathrm{Cat}}\circ\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}\colon G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}\to\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}\,.

(7.5)

Proof.

We have a natural (naturality here and below refers to
(X,\tau)
in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
) commutative diagram of
C^{*}
-categories

\textstyle{\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}(X\otimes G_{can,max})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{\bar{C}}_{\mathrm{lf}}^{G,\mathrm{ctr}}({\mathcal{O}}_{\tau}(X)\otimes G_{can,max})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{C}}_{\tau}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{{\mathbf{D}}_{\tau}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{0}

(7.6)

where the top horizontal and left vertical morphisms are induced from canonical inclusions of bornological coarse spaces.

We now use that
K^{C^{*}\mathrm{Cat}}
sends exact sequences in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
to fibre sequences in
\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}
(
[
BEL
, Thm. 1.32.5]
or
[

, Prop.14.7]
). We apply
K^{C^{*}\mathrm{Cat}}
to Diagram (

) and get a natural morphism of fibre sequences

Consider
Y
in
G\mathbf{LCH}^{\mathrm{prop}}_{+}
.
At various places we will use the following properties of this functor.

Lemma 7.2
.

If
Y
is homotopy equivalent to a
G
-finite
G
-CW-complex with finite stabilizers, then:

1.

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(Y),-)
sends exact sequences in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
to fibre sequences.

2.

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(Y),-)
annihilates flasque objects in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
.

3.

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(Y),-)
sends relative Morita equivalences to equivalences.

Proof.

By
[
BEL
, Prop. 1.26]
the object
\mathrm{kk}^{G}(C_{0}(Y))
is
G
-proper and therefore
\mathrm{ind}
-
G
-proper in the sense of
[
BEL
, Def. 1.25]
.
The assertions now follow from
[
BEL
, Thm. 1.32]
.
∎

In the present paper
\otimes
denotes the maximal tensor product of
C^{*}
-categories
[
BEL
, Def. 7.2]
. By
[
BEL
, Prop. 1.21]
the stable
\infty
-category category
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
has a presentably symmetric monoidal
structure induced by the maximal tensor product of
C^{*}
-algebras, and by
[
BEL
, Thm. 1.32]
the functor

\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
. has a symmetric monoidal refinement. We define the functor

-\hatotimes-\colon{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\times\mathrm{KK}\xrightarrow{\mathrm{kk}^{G}\times\mathrm{Res}^{\{1\}}_{G}}\mathrm{KK}^{G}\times\mathrm{KK}^{G}\xrightarrow{\otimes}\mathrm{KK}^{G}\,,

(7.18)

where
\otimes
is structure map of the symmetric monoidal structure of
\mathrm{KK}^{G}

and
\mathrm{Res}^{\{1\}}_{G}
is the restriction induced by the projection
G\to\{1\}
from
[
BEL
, Thm. 1.22]
(on
C^{*}
-algebras
\mathrm{Res}^{\{1\}}_{G}
is given by equipping a
C^{*}
-algebra with the trivial
G
-action).
Using that
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
is presentably symmetric monoidal category and
\mathrm{Res}^{\{1\}}_{G}
preserves
small colimits we see that
\hatotimes
preserves
small colimits in its second variable.

Let
A
be in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg})
and
\mathbf{Q}
be in
C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}
.

Lemma 7.3
.

We have an equivalence

A\hatotimes\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathbf{Q})\simeq\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(A\otimes\mathrm{Res}^{\{1\}}_{G}(\mathbf{Q}))

which is natural in
A
and
\mathbf{Q}
.

Proof.

The chain of natural equivalences

\displaystyle A\hatotimes\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathbf{Q})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{def.}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(A)\otimes\mathrm{Res}^{\{1\}}_{G}(\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathbf{Q}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptsize{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{KKG}{}{}, \text{Thm.\,1.22}]}}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(A)\otimes\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(\mathrm{Res}^{\{1\}}_{G}(\mathbf{Q}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\scriptsize{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{KKG}{}{}, \text{Thm.\,1.32}]}}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(A\otimes\mathrm{Res}^{\{1\}}_{G}(\mathbf{Q}))

gives the desired equivalence.
∎

From now on, in order to simplify the notation, we will write
\mathbf{Q}
instead of
\mathrm{Res}^{\{1\}}_{G}(\mathbf{Q})
.

8
Naturality of the Paschke morphism

In this subsection we discuss the naturality of the Paschke morphism from Definition
7.4
.
More precisely, we will construct a natural transformation whose component on
(X,\tau)
is the Paschke morphism of Definition
7.4
.
Note that naturality in the
\infty
-categorical sense is more than the existence of a filler for the square

\textstyle{K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}(\iota^{\mathrm{scl}}(X,\tau))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\scriptstyle{p_{(X,\tau)}}
\textstyle{K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}(\iota^{\mathrm{scl}}(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{p_{(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})}}
\textstyle{K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}(\iota^{\mathrm{top}}(X,\tau))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f_{*}}
\textstyle{K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}(\iota^{\mathrm{top}}(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))}

(8.1)

for all morphisms
f\colon(X,\tau)\to(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
.
The existence of such a filler can indeed be easily seen by considering the big diagram (
8.2
) below.
In order to produce the data of a natural transformation we must reformulate the construction
of the Paschke morphisms appropriately.
The main problem is that
\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(X)\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X))
is not a functor on
(X,\tau)
so that
\delta_{(X,\tau)}
and
\mu_{(X,\tau)}
can not be interpreted as natural transformations separately.

We assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
is effectively additive and admits countable AV-sums.
In order to get an idea what we have to do to get the existence of a filler of (
8.1
) we first consider the diagram

\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}(-,\mathbf{Q}(f))}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{(X,\tau)}}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(X)\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}(f^{*},-)}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{(X,\tau)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(X),\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{std}}^{(G)})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}(f^{*},-)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(X^{\prime}),C_{0}(X)\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{(X,\tau)}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(X^{\prime}),C_{0}(X^{\prime})\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}(-,f^{*})}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}(-,\mathbf{Q}(f)}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},\mathbf{Q}_{\tau^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\delta_{(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(X^{\prime}),C_{0}(X^{\prime})\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}))\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\mu_{(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})}}
\textstyle{\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(X^{\prime}),\mathbf{Q}_{\mathrm{std}}^{(G)})}

(8.2)

all of whose cells have essentially obvious fillers. This already implies that the Paschke morphism is natural on the level of homotopy categories.

Remark 8.1
.

Our idea for showing that the Paschke morphism is an equivalence is to reduce this by homotopy invariance to
G
-simplicial complexes, and then by excision to
G
-orbits where it can be verified by an explicit calculation.
The excision step requires a natural transformation on the spectrum level.
If one is only interested in homotopy groups, then it would be sufficient to know the compatibility
of the Paschke map with the Mayer–Vietoris boundary maps which is an immediate consequence of the
spectrum-valued naturality.
So even if we were finally only interested in the Paschke isomorphism on the level of homotopy groups
we would still need the spectrum level natural transformation for the proof that it is an isomorphism.

For similar reasons, the spectrum-valued version is also crucial in the proof of our second Theorem
1.8

comparing the two assembly maps, though the latter is indeed a statement on the level of homotopy groups.

∎

In the following remarks about general
\infty
-categorical constructions we prepare the actual construction of the natural Paschke transformation.

Remark 8.2
.

For a category
{\mathcal{C}}
let
\mathbf{Tw}({\mathcal{C}})
denote the twisted arrow category.
Objects are morphisms
C\to C^{\prime}
in
{\mathcal{C}}
, and morphisms
(C_{0}\to C_{0}^{\prime})\to(C_{1}\to C_{1}^{\prime})
are commutative diagrams

\textstyle{C_{0}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{0}^{\prime}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{1}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\textstyle{C_{1}^{\prime}}

(8.3)

We have a canonical functor

({\mathtt{ev}},{\mathtt{ev}}^{\prime})\colon\mathbf{Tw}({\mathcal{C}})\to{\mathcal{C}}^{\mathrm{op}}\times{\mathcal{C}}\,,\quad(C\to C^{\prime})\mapsto(C,C^{\prime})\,.

If
F,G\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to{\mathcal{D}}
are two functors to a stable
\infty
-category, then we can express the spectrum of natural transformations
between
F
and
G
as

\mathrm{nat}(F,G)\simeq\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{\mathbf{Tw}({\mathcal{C}})}{\mathtt{map}}_{{\mathcal{D}}}(F\circ{\mathtt{ev}},G\circ{\mathtt{ev}}^{\prime})\,.

(8.4)

We refer to
[
GHN17
,
Gla16
]
where this is discussed even in the more general case of
{\mathcal{C}}
being an
\infty
-category.

∎

Remark 8.3
.

Recall that our universe in which we do homotopy theory is the one of small sets.
The corresponding categories then belong to the large universe.
Let
{\mathcal{C}}
be a locally small, large presentable stable
\infty
-category and fix an object
C_{0}
in
{\mathcal{C}}
.
Then
C_{0}
is
\kappa
-compact for some
small ordinal
\kappa
.
The mapping space functor

{\mathtt{map}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}(C_{0},-)\colon{\mathcal{C}}\to\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}
preserves small limits and
\kappa
-filtered colimits. Consequently, it is the right adjoint in an adjunction

(E\mapsto C_{0}\wedge E):\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}\leftrightarrows{\mathcal{C}}:(C\mapsto{\mathtt{map}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}(C_{0},C))\,.

(8.5)

The left adjoint functor is essentially uniquely characterized by an equivalence
C_{0}\simeq C_{0}\wedge S
, where
S
is the sphere spectrum, and the fact that it preserves small colimits. This left adjoint also depends functorially on
C_{0}
.
In fact, we have a functor

{\mathcal{C}}\times\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}\to{\mathcal{C}}\,,\quad(C,E)\mapsto C\wedge E

(8.6)

which is essentially uniquely characterized by a natural equivalence

C\simeq C\wedge S

(8.7)

and the fact that it preserves small colimits in the second variable.

The counit of the adjunction in (
8.5
) is a natural transformation

C_{0}\wedge{\mathtt{map}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}(C_{0},-)\to{\mathtt{id}}_{{\mathcal{C}}}(-)

(8.8)

of endofunctors of
{\mathcal{C}}
.

∎

Remark 8.4
.

Let
{\mathcal{C}},{\mathcal{D}},{\mathcal{E}}
be
\infty
-categories and
-\hatotimes-\colon{\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathcal{D}}\to{\mathcal{E}}
be a functor. We consider
\infty
-categories
{\mathcal{I}}
,
\mathcal{J}
and
natural transformations of functors
{(F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha}}{{\to}}F^{\prime})}\colon{\mathcal{I}}\to{\mathcal{C}}
and
{(G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\beta}}{{\to}}G^{\prime})}\colon\mathcal{J}\to{\mathcal{D}}
.
Then we get a natural transformation of functors

{(F\times G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\times\beta}}{{\to}}F^{\prime}\times G^{\prime})}\colon{\mathcal{I}}\times\mathcal{J}\to{\mathcal{C}}\times{\mathcal{D}}\,,

and by composition with
-\hatotimes-
a natural transformation

{(F\hatotimes G\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\alpha\hatotimes\beta}}{{\to}}F^{\prime}\hatotimes G^{\prime})}\colon{\mathcal{I}}\times\mathcal{J}\to{\mathcal{E}}\,,

(8.9)

where we write

F\hatotimes G
for
(-\hatotimes-)\circ(F\times G)
.

∎

Applying (
8.6
) to
{\mathcal{C}}=\mathrm{KK}
we get a functor

(B,E)\mapsto B\wedge E\colon\mathrm{KK}\times\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}\to\mathrm{KK}\,.

In the following we specialize
B
to
\mathrm{kk}({\mathbb{C}})
.
We then have a functor
(A,E)\mapsto A\wedge E
given as the composition

{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\times\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}\xrightarrow{{\mathtt{id}}\times{(\mathrm{kk}({\mathbb{C}})\wedge-)}}{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\times\mathrm{KK}\xrightarrow{-\hatotimes-}\mathrm{KK}^{G}\,,

(8.10)

where
\hat{\otimes}
is as in (
7.18
).
Note that

A\wedge S\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{qewfpojfopqwfqewfeqf}}}{{\simeq}}A\hatotimes{(\mathrm{kk}({\mathbb{C}})\wedge S)}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{qwfqewfqewfqewfqewffefefqewefefdfqef}}}{{\simeq}}A\hatotimes\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}({\mathbb{C}})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle Lem.\ref{glkbijowerfvvfevsdfvsfdv}}}{{\simeq}}\mathrm{kk}^{G}(A\otimes{\mathrm{Res}^{G}_{\{1\}}({\mathbb{C}})})\simeq\mathrm{kk}^{G}(A)\,.

Since the functor
-\hat{\otimes}-
in (
7.18
) preserves small colimits in its second variable, the functor in (
8.10
) is essentially uniquely determined by
the equivalence

A\wedge S\simeq\mathrm{kk}^{G}(A)
and the fact that it preserves small colimits in the second variable.
Furthermore, by the adjunction (
8.5
) we have a natural equivalence

{\mathtt{map}}_{\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}}(E,\mathrm{KK}^{G}(A,B))\simeq\mathrm{KK}^{G}(A\wedge E,B)

(8.11)

for
E
in
\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}
,
A
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})
, and
B
in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
.

We consider the functor

F\colon(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}\xrightarrow{C_{0}(-)\times\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},-)}{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\times\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}\xrightarrow{-\wedge-,\eqref{qewfpojfopqwfqewfeqf}}\mathrm{KK}^{G}

(8.12)

written as

((X,\tau),B)\mapsto C_{0}(X)\wedge\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},B)\,.

We further consider the functor

H\colon(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}\xrightarrow{C_{0}(-)\times{\mathtt{id}}(-)}{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}})\times\mathrm{KK}\xrightarrow{-\hatotimes-}\mathrm{KK}^{G}

written as

((X,\tau),B)\mapsto C_{0}(X)\hatotimes B\,.

We now construct the diagonal
transformation

{(F\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\tilde{\delta}}}{{\to}}H)}\colon(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}\,.

(8.13)

Its specialization at
(X,\tau)
in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
and
B
in
\mathrm{KK}
is a morphism

\tilde{\delta}_{(X,\tau),B}\colon C_{0}(X)\wedge\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},B)\to C_{0}(X)\hatotimes B

(8.14)

in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
. Inserting (
8.10
) into the definition (
8.12
) of
F

we get

F=(-\hatotimes-)\circ(C_{0}(-)\times\mathrm{kk}({\mathbb{C}})\wedge\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},-))\,.

We now obtain
\tilde{\delta}
in (
8.13
) by specializing (
8.9
) to the transformations

{(C_{0}(-)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle{\mathtt{id}}}}{{\to}}C_{0}(-))}\colon(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Alg}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{sep}})

and

(\mathrm{kk}({\mathbb{C}})\wedge\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},-)\to{\mathtt{id}}(-))\colon\mathrm{KK}\to\mathrm{KK}

given by (
8.8
).

We define the functor

Q\colon G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}\to\mathrm{KK}\,,\quad(X,\tau)\mapsto Q_{\tau}(X)\coloneqq\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}(\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X))\,,

(8.15)

see (
7.4
) for
\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)
.
Then we consider the functor

\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}\times\mathrm{KK}\,,\quad((X,\tau)\to(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))\mapsto(X^{\prime},Q_{\tau}(X))\,.

(8.16)

The pull-back of
\tilde{\delta}
in (
8.13
) along (
8.16
) yields
a natural transformation

(\delta\colon C_{0}(-^{\prime})\wedge\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q(-))\to C_{0}(-^{\prime})\hatotimes Q(-)):\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}

(8.17)

whose evaluation at an object
f\colon(X,\tau)\to(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
in
\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}
is a morphism

\delta_{f}\colon C_{0}(X^{\prime})\wedge\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q_{\tau}(X))\to C_{0}(X^{\prime})\hatotimes Q_{\tau}(X)

(8.18)

in
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
.
This is our version of the diagonal (
7.20
) as a natural transformation. In fact, under the canonical equivalence

\displaystyle\mathrm{KK}^{G}

\displaystyle(C_{0}(X)\wedge\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q_{\tau}(X)),C_{0}({X})\hatotimes Q_{{\tau}}(X))

(8.19)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{wergoihweriogergrwfere}}}{{\simeq}}{\mathtt{map}}(\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},\mathbf{Q}_{{\tau}}(X)),\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(X),C_{0}(X)\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X))

the map
\delta_{{\mathtt{id}}_{(X,\tau)}}
in (
8.18
) corresponds to
\delta_{(X,\tau)}
from (
7.20
).

We now construct the refinement (
8.24
) of the family of multiplication maps
\mu_{(X,\tau)}
from (
7.19
) for all
(X,\tau)
in

G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
. We start with the functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces((X,\tau)\to(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime}))}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(X^{\prime}){\otimes}\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)\,.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces C_{0}(-^{\prime})\otimes\mathbf{Q}(-)}

We also consider
{\mathbf{Q}}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}
as a constant functor from
\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}
to
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
. We first construct
a natural transformation

(\tilde{\mu}\colon C_{0}(-^{\prime})\otimes\mathbf{Q}(-)\to\mathbf{Q}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}})\colon\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})\,.

(8.20)

For every object
f\colon(X,\tau)\to(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
in
\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}
we must define a functor

\tilde{\mu}_{f}\colon C_{0}(X^{\prime})\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)\to\mathbf{Q}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}\,.

(8.21)

This construction extends the construction of
{\mu_{(X,\tau)}}
in (
7.19
) which will be
recovered as
\mu_{(X,\tau)}=\tilde{\mu}_{{\mathtt{id}}_{(X,\tau)}}
.

1.

objects: The functor
\tilde{\mu}_{f}
sends the object
(C,\rho,\mu)
in
C_{0}({X^{\prime}})\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)
(hence an object of
\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)
) to the object
(C,\rho)
in
\mathbf{Q}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}
.

2.

morphisms: If
[A]\colon(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\to(C,\rho,\mu)
is a morphism in
\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)
and
h
is in
C_{0}({X^{\prime}})
, then

\tilde{\mu}_{f}(h\otimes[A])\coloneqq[\phi(f^{*}h)A]
, see (
6.1
) for the definition of
\phi
.

The argument that the functor
\tilde{\mu}_{f}
is well-defined is the same as for
{\mu_{(X,\tau)}}
.
We now check that
\tilde{\mu}\coloneqq({\tilde{\mu}}_{f})_{f\in\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}}
is a natural transformation.
We consider a morphism
f\to g
in
\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}
, see (
8.3
). Since we work with the opposite of the twisted arrow category, it is given by a commutative diagram

\textstyle{(X,\tau)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{f}
\scriptstyle{\alpha}
\textstyle{(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})}
\textstyle{(Y,\sigma)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{(Y^{\prime},\sigma^{\prime})\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta}

(8.22)

We must show that

\textstyle{C_{0}(X^{\prime})\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\beta^{*}\otimes\mathbf{Q}(\alpha)}
\scriptstyle{{\tilde{\mu}}_{f}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces C_{0}(Y^{\prime})\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\sigma}(Y)}
\scriptstyle{{\tilde{\mu}}_{g}}
\textstyle{\mathbf{Q}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}}

commutes.

1.

objects: Let
(C,\rho,\mu)
be an object in
C_{0}(X^{\prime})\otimes\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)
. Then we have the equality

{\tilde{\mu}}_{g}((\beta^{*}\otimes\mathbf{Q}(\alpha))(C,\rho,\mu))={\tilde{\mu}}_{g}(C,\rho,{\alpha}_{*}\mu)=(C,\rho)={\tilde{\mu}}_{f}(C,\rho,\mu)\,.

2.

morphisms: Let
[A]\colon(C^{\prime},\rho^{\prime},\mu^{\prime})\to(C,\rho,\mu)
be a morphism in
\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X)
and
h
be in
C_{0}(X^{\prime})
.
Then we have the equality

{\tilde{\mu}}_{g}((\beta^{*}\otimes\mathbf{Q}(\alpha))(h\otimes[A]))={\tilde{\mu}}_{g}(\beta^{*}h\otimes[\alpha_{*}A])={[\phi(g^{*}(\beta^{*}(h)))\alpha_{*}A]}=[(\alpha_{*}\phi)(g^{*}\beta^{*}h)A]\,.

On the other hand,

{\tilde{\mu}}_{f}(h\otimes[A])=[\phi({f^{*}}h)A]\,.

The desired equality

[\phi({f^{*}}h)A]=[(\alpha_{*}\phi)({g^{*}}\beta^{*}h)A]

now follows from the identity

(\alpha_{*}\phi)(g^{*}\beta^{*}h)=\phi(\alpha^{*}g^{*}\beta^{*}h)=\phi(f^{*}h)

since

\alpha^{*}g^{*}\beta^{*}h=f^{*}h
by the commutativity of (
8.22
).

We post-compose the transformation in (
8.20
) with the functor
\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}
and get a natural transformation

(\mathrm{kk}^{G}(\tilde{\mu})\colon\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(C_{0}(-^{\prime})\otimes\mathbf{Q}(-))\to Q^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}})\colon\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}\,,

(8.23)

where we use the abbreviation

Q^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}\coloneqq\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(\mathbf{Q}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}})\,.

Composing the transformation (
8.23
)
with the equivalence

C_{0}(-^{\prime})\hatotimes Q(-)\simeq\mathrm{kk}_{C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}}^{G}(C_{0}(-^{\prime})\otimes\mathbf{Q}(-))

given by Lemma
7.3

(see (
8.15
) for the notation
Q(-)
) we get a natural transformation

(\mu\colon C_{0}(-^{\prime})\hatotimes Q(-)\to\tilde{Q}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}})\colon\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}\,.

(8.24)

The composition of (
8.17
) and (
8.24
)
then gives a natural transformation

(\mu\circ\delta\colon C_{0}(-^{\prime})\wedge\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q(-))\to{C_{0}(-^{\prime})\otimes Q(-)}\to Q^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}})\colon\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}

whose value at the object
f\colon(X,\tau)\to(X^{\prime},\tau^{\prime})
is the morphism

\mu_{f}\circ\delta_{f}\colon C_{0}(X^{\prime})\wedge\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q_{\tau}(X))\to{C_{0}(X^{\prime})\otimes Q_{\tau}(X)}\to Q^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}\,.

Equivalently, by (
8.4
) and since the target functor is constant we can interpret
this as a map of spectra

S\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}}C_{0}(-^{\prime})\wedge\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q(-)),Q^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}})\,.

(8.25)

Note that
\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}
is small and the presentable category
\mathrm{KK}^{G}
admits all small colimits.
We now use the chain of canonical equivalences

\displaystyle\mathrm{KK}^{G}(\operatorname*{\mathtt{colim}}_{\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})^{\mathrm{op}}}C_{0}(-^{\prime})\wedge\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q(-)),Q^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}})

\displaystyle\simeq

\displaystyle\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})}\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(-^{\prime})\wedge\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q(-)),Q^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{wergoihweriogergrwfere}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\operatorname*{\mathtt{lim}}_{\mathbf{Tw}(G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}})}{\mathtt{map}}(\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q(-)),\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(-^{\prime}),Q^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}))

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{wetliogjwoiergregregef}}}{{\simeq}}

\displaystyle\mathrm{nat}(\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q(-)),\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(-),Q^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}))\,,

where
\mathrm{nat}
denotes the spectrum of natural transformations between functors from

G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
to
\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}
.
Therefore (
8.25
) provides a map

S\to\mathrm{nat}(\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q(-)),\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(-),Q^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}}))\,.

This is the desired natural transformation

p\colon\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q(-))\to\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(-),Q^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}})

(8.26)

of functors from

G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
to
\mathbf{Sp}^{\mathrm{}}
.
It follows from the identifications of
\delta_{{\mathtt{id}}_{(X,\tau)}}
with
\delta_{(X,\tau)}
by (
8.19
) and of
{\tilde{\mu}}_{{\mathtt{id}}_{(X,\tau)}}
with
\mu_{(X,\tau)}
stated after (
8.21
) that the evaluation of
p
at
X
in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
is equivalent to the morphism

p_{(X,\tau)}
from (
7.21
).

Recall that we use the notation

\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},Q_{\tau}(X))\simeq\mathrm{KK}({\mathbb{C}},\mathbf{Q}_{\tau}(X))\simeq K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}(\iota^{\mathrm{scl}}(X,\tau))\,,

and

\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(X),Q^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}})\simeq\mathrm{KK}^{G}(C_{0}(X),\mathbf{Q}^{(G)}_{\mathrm{std}})\simeq K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}(\iota^{\mathrm{top}}(X,\tau))\,.

Therefore (
8.26
) is the desired Paschke transformation

p\colon K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}\circ\iota^{\mathrm{scl}}\to K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}\circ\iota^{\mathrm{top}}\,.

By construction, we see that the Paschke transformation is natural in the coefficient category
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}}_{\mathrm{ndeg},\mathrm{eadd},\omega\mathrm{add}})
.
This finishes the proof of Theorem
1.4
.
1
.

9
Reduction to
\bm{G}
-orbits

In this section we reduce the verification of Theorems
1.4
.
2
and
1.4
.
3
to the case of
G
-orbits.
A discrete
G
-uniform bornological coarse space is a
G
-set with
the minimal coarse and bornological structures and the discrete uniform structure.
An object
Y
of
G{\mathbf{Set}}
can canonically be considered as a discrete object in
G\mathbf{UBC}
which we will also denote by
Y
. Alternatively we may use the more informative, but lengthier notation
Y_{min,min,disc}
, where the first
min
indicates the minimal coarse structure, the second
min
the minimal bornology, and finally
disc
the discrete uniform structure.
Note that the construction
Y\mapsto Y_{min,min,disc}
is functorial only for maps between
G
-sets with finite fibres.

Let

\phi\colon{\mathbb{R}}\to(0,\infty)
be a bounded function such that
\phi_{|(-\infty,0]}\equiv 1
.
If
Y
is in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
, then we can consider the scale

\tau_{disc}\coloneqq(\phi,\psi_{disc})
on
Y
, where

\psi_{disc}(t)\coloneqq\begin{cases}Y\times Y&\text{for }t\leq 0\,,\\
{\mathtt{diag}}(Y)&\text{for }t>0\,.\end{cases}

(9.1)

Recall the Definition
5.6
of
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
.

Definition 9.1
.

We say that
\phi
is good if
(\{*\},\tau_{disc})
belongs to
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
.

For example, the constant function
\phi\equiv 1
is good.

Assume that
(X,\tau)
is in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
with

\tau=(\phi,\psi)
. Let furthermore
Y
be in
G{\mathbf{Set}}
.

Lemma 9.2
.

1.

If
X
is not empty, then
\phi
is good.

2.

If
Y
is countable and
\phi
is good, then
(Y,\tau_{disc})
belongs to
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
.

Proof.

For the first assertion
the only non-trivial condition to check is that
(*,(\phi,\tau_{disc}))
satisfies the condition of Definition
5.6
.
1c
.

Since
X
is not empty we can choose a point
x
in
X
and consider the
G
-invariant subset
{A}\coloneqq Gx
. We consider
A
as an object in
G\mathbf{UBC}
, with structures induced from
X
. By Lemma
5.12
we have
({A},\tau_{{A}})\in G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
, where
\tau_{{A}}
is the restriction of the scale
\tau
to
{A}
. The first entry of

\tau_{{A}}
is still
\phi
. By definition of
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
, we can choose a
G
-invariant locally uniformly finite subset
L
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{{A}}}({A})\otimes G_{can,max}
such that
L\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{{A}}}({A})\otimes G_{can,max}
is a coarse equivalence. We consider the subset

\bar{L}:={\mathtt{pr}}_{[0,\infty)}(L\cap([0,\infty)\times\{x\}\times G))\times G

of
{\mathcal{O}}_{(\phi,\psi_{disc})}(*)\otimes G_{can,max}
and observe the following facts:

1.

The subset
\bar{L}
is
G
-invariant.

2.

The inclusion
\bar{L}\to{\mathcal{O}}_{(\phi,\psi_{disc})}(*)\otimes G_{can,max}
is a coarse equivalence.
We claim that
\bar{L}
is coarsely dense. Since
G
acts freely on
{\mathcal{O}}_{(\phi,\psi_{disc})}(*)\otimes G_{can,max}
the claim implies the assertion.
We now show the claim. We can choose a coarse entourage

U
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{{A}}}({A})\otimes G_{can,max}
such that
L
is
U
-dense.
After enlarging
U
if necessary can assume that it is of the form
V\times W
for a coarse entourage
V
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{{A}}}({A})

and a coarse entourage
W
of
G_{can,max}
which contains the diagonal.
We obtain a coarse entourage

\bar{U}:={\mathtt{pr}}_{[0,\infty)\times G}(U)
of
{\mathcal{O}}_{(\phi,\psi_{disc})}(*)\otimes G_{can,max}
. We claim that
\bar{L}
is
\bar{U}
-dense.
Let
(t,g)
be in
[0,\infty)\times G
. Then there exists

(s,y,h)
in
L
such that
(t,x,g)\in U[(s,y,h)]
. Then
(s,g)\in\bar{L}
and

(t,g)\in\bar{U}[(s,g)]
.

3.

We claim that
\bar{L}
is locally uniformly finite. Let
U
and
\bar{U}
be as in
2
.
After enlarging
U
we can assume that
\bar{U}\times{\mathtt{diag}}(A)\subseteq U
.
Let
(t,g)
be in
[0,\infty)\times G
. If
(s,h)\in\bar{U}[(t,g)]\cap\bar{L}
, then
(s,x,h)\in U[(t,x,g)]\cap L
.
Hence
|\bar{U}[(t,g)]\cap\bar{L}|\leq|U[(t,x,g)]\cap L|
and therefore

\sup_{(t,g)\in[0,\infty)\times G}|\bar{U}[(t,g)]\cap\bar{L}|\leq\sup_{(t,g)\in[0,\infty)\times G}|U[(t,x,g)]\cap L|<\infty\,,

where the second inequality holds since
L
is locally uniformly finite in
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{{A}}}({A})\otimes G_{can,max}
and
\{x\}
is bounded in
{A}
.
Since any entourage of
{\mathcal{O}}_{(\phi,\psi_{disc})}(*)\otimes G_{can,max}
is contained in an entourage of the form
\bar{U}
this shows the claim.

We can now conclude that
\phi
is good.

We now show the second assertion. We must assume that
Y
is countable in order to ensure that
Y_{disc}
is second countable required by Condition
5.6
.
1a
. In order to verify Condition
5.6
.
1c
let
\bar{L}
be a
G
-invariant locally uniformly finite subset of

{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{disc}}(*)\otimes G_{can,max}
. Then the preimage
L\coloneqq{\mathtt{pr}}^{-1}_{[0,\infty)\times G}(\bar{L})
is a
G
-invariant locally uniformly finite subset of
{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{disc}}(Y)\otimes G_{can,max}
such that
L\to{\mathcal{O}}_{\tau_{disc}}(Y)\otimes G_{can,max}
is a coarse equivalence.
∎

Let
{\mathcal{F}}
denote a family of subgroups of
G
. We will be mainly interested in the family
\mathbf{Fin}
of finite subgroups, but the following proposition is valid for any family
{\mathcal{F}}
. We let
G_{{\mathcal{F}}}{\mathbf{Set}}
be the category of very small
G
-sets with stabilizers in
{\mathcal{F}}
.

Let
(X,\tau)
be in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
. We write
\tau=(\phi,\psi)
.
Below we use the first entry
\phi
in order to form
\tau_{disc}
.
We assume that
{\mathbf{C}}
in
{\mathbf{Fun}}(BG,C^{*}\mathbf{Cat}^{\mathrm{nu}})
is effectively additive and admits countable AV-sums and recall the Definition
7.4
of the Paschke morphism.

Proposition 9.3
.

Assume:

1.

The Paschke morphism for
(S,\tau_{disc})
is an equivalence for every

S
in
G_{{\mathcal{F}}}\mathbf{Orb}
.

2.

X
is homotopy equivalent to a
G
-finite
G
-simplicial complex with stabilizers in
{\mathcal{F}}
and with structures induced by its spherical path metrics.

Then the Paschke morphism for
(X,\tau)
is an equivalence.

Proof.

The case of an empty
X
is obvious. We therefore assume that
X
is not empty and argue by induction on the dimension
n
of the
G
-simplicial complex in Assumption
9.3
.
2
.
We assume that the first component of all scales below is given by
\phi
which is good by Lemma
9.2
.
1
.

Assume that
d=0
and that
(K,\tau_{K})
is in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}

such that
K
is a
0
-dimensional
G
-finite
G
-simplicial complex with stabilizers in
{\mathcal{F}}
. Then by an inspection of the constructions we have
p_{(K,\tau_{K})}\simeq p_{(K,\tau_{disc})}
.

For every orbit
S
in
G\backslash K
we consider the closed invariant partition
(S,K\setminus S)
of
K
.
Applying excision for the functors
K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}
and
K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}

we get the respective projections

q^{{\mathcal{X}}}_{S}\colon K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}(K)\to K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}(S)

and

q^{{\mathrm{An}}}_{S}\colon K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}(K)\to K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}(S)
for all
S
in
G\backslash K
.
We have a commutative square

\textstyle{K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\oplus_{S}q^{{\mathcal{X}}}_{S}}
\scriptstyle{p_{(K,\tau_{K})}}
\textstyle{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\bigoplus_{S\in G\backslash K}K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}(S)}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\oplus_{S}p_{(S,\tau_{disc})}}
\textstyle{K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{\oplus_{S}q^{\mathrm{An}}_{S}}
\textstyle{\bigoplus_{S\in G\backslash K}K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}(S)}

Since we assume that
G\backslash K
is finite the horizontal morphisms are equivalences by excision. Furthermore, the right vertical morphism is an equivalence by Assumption
9.3
.
1
. Consequently, the left vertical morphism is an equivalence.

Let
n
be in
{\rm Nature}
and assume that we have shown that
p_{(K,\tau_{K})}
is an equivalence provided
K
is
G
-finite
G
-simplicial complex of dimension
n
with stabilizers in
{\mathcal{F}}
and with structures induced by its spherical path metrics.
Let then
(X,\tau)
be in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
and
assume that there exists a homotopy equivalence

(X,\tau)\to(K,\tau_{K})
. By the naturality of the Paschke transformation
we can consider the commutative square

\textstyle{K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{p_{(X,\tau)}}
\textstyle{K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}(K)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\scriptstyle{p_{(K,\tau_{K})}}
\textstyle{K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}(X)\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}
\scriptstyle{\simeq}
\textstyle{K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}(K)}

Since the functors
K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,{\mathcal{X}}}
and
K_{{\mathbf{C}}}^{G,\mathrm{An}}
are homotopy invariant by
[
BEL
, Thm. 1.15]
and Proposition
4.10
, respectively, for the notion of homotopy from Definition
5.13
,
the horizontal morphisms are equivalences. By assumption the right vertical morphism in an equivalence, too. Consequently, the left vertical morphism is also an equivalence.

We now show the induction step.
Assume that
(K,\tau_{K})
in
G\mathbf{UBC}^{\mathrm{scl}}
is such that
K
is a
G
-finite
G
-simplicial complex of dimension
n
with stabilizers in
{\mathcal{F}}
with structures induced by its spherical path metrics.
Let
Y
be the closed
1/2
-neighbourhood of the
(n-1)
-skeleton
